II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
10 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your rebuttal testimony? 
11 
A. 
The purpose of my rebuttal testimony is to respond to certain recommendations in the 
12

13

14

the material differences between the Company’s corrections and updates gas 
15

16

Staff’s concern that the Company omitted the most recent available economic 
17 
data in preparing its corrections and updates forecast; 
18

19 
explanatory independent variables; and 
20 
iv. 
Staff’s opinions that it is more appropriate to base the Company’s definition of 
21 
normal weather on ten years of weather data rather than 30 years, and that a ten-

III. 
The Company’s Response to Staff’s Testimony 
14 
Q. 
Are the differences between your forecast and Staff’s forecast material? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  Although the overall revenue impact is small, a gas deliveries forecast should 
16 
properly assign forecast volumes to various rate groups for the development of the 
17 
appropriate price signals to customers.  In Staff’s forecast, her overreliance on certain 
18 
variables puts too much weight on the assumption that the forecast period beginning in 
19 
mid-2020 will be similar to the historical period prior to mid-2020.  The COVID-19 
20 
pandemic’s effect on the economy ensures that the future will not be like the past in 
21 
certain relevant respects.  A more detailed description of the differences in the Company 
22 
and Staff’s forecasts can be found in Exhibit __ (TEP-1R). 
23 


--- Page 5 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
Page 3 of 7 
Q.
Staff  (at 4) expresses concern that you omitted the most recent available economic 
1 
data in preparing your forecast.  Do you believe that this is a reason for concern? 
2 
A.
While the Company agrees that using the most-recently available economic input data is 
3 
most appropriate for use in its modeling, with a COVID-19 vaccine now approaching 
4 
distribution, the Company believes that its forecast is a comprehensive model of the 
5 
anticipated recovery that accurately forecasts natural gas requirements for the Rate Year 
6 
and the Data Years.  As the pandemic has evolved, the Moody’s forecasts have also 
7 
evolved.  In Figure 1 below, the Company shows the Moody’s forecasts for three of the 
8 
key drivers in its forecasts:  regional households, Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”), and 
9 
employment.  In each chart, the Company shows the April 2020 Moody’s forecast that it 
10 
used to develop its corrections and updates forecasting models, the September 2020 
11 
Moody’s forecast used by Staff to develop its forecast, and the most recent October 
12 
2020 Moody’s forecast.  As can be seen, between September 2020 and October 2020, 
13 
Moody’s forecasts for households and GDP have increased to levels at or above the 
14 
April 2020 forecast.  Only its employment forecast for October 2020 remains lower than 
15 
the April 2020 forecast.  Thus, on balance, the use of April 2020 Moody’s forecast data 
16 
is more in line with Moody’s current forecast than the September 2020 data utilized by 
17 
Staff. 
18

Rebuttal Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
Page 5 of 7 
Q.
Staff also expresses concern (at 9) that several of the Company’s delivery models 
1 
lack relevant explanatory independent variables other than weather.  Do you 
2 
agree? 
3 
A.
I disagree.  Out of the 30 models that the Company produced for its forecast (ten meter 
4 
count models and 20 use-per-customer models), Staff pointed out only three models that 
5 
relied on weather as the only explanatory variable and one model that did not rely on an 
6 
econometric model.  These four models were all use-per-customer models.   It is 
7 
noteworthy that after criticizing the Company’s models without economic variables, she 
8 
then produced two herself:  the Residential Heating meter count models for East Gate 
9 
and West Gate.  
10 
11 
Staff’s forecast is an incomplete forecast with apparent weaknesses.  As I describe in 
12 
more detail in Exhibit __ (TEP-1R), Staff failed to use its methodology to forecast all 
13 
Service Classes and demonstrate a complete forecast, choosing instead to use some of 
14 
their own and some of the Company’s models.  Staff’s forecast reflects a number of 
15 
choices that erode the model’s ability to predict future deliveries accurately.  The 
16 
forecast:  lacks sufficient variables to explain changes in meter counts, relying solely on 
17 
backward looking (pre-pandemic) trends; comingles peak versus off-peak usage rather 
18 
than modeling these separately; and, for commercial customers and the other customer 
19 
group, omits an important step of separately modeling use per customer and meter count 
20 
before deriving the volume of gas delivered.  Staff also added terms to their models that 
21 
the Company considers inappropriate when forecasting the Rate Year and Data Years.   
22 


--- Page 8 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
Page 6 of 7 
Q.
Staff also asserts (at 17-18) that it is more appropriate to base the forecast on ten 
1 
years of weather data rather than 30 years because a ten-year average weather 
2 
normalization more reasonably reflects anticipated weather trends relevant to 
3 
forecasting billed sales.  Do you have any comments concerning this claim? 
4 
A.
If one assumes that rising average temperatures, both globally and regionally in the 
5 
Upstate New York area, will continue in the future, then using only the most recent ten 
6 
years of weather data to define normal weather could lead to a better approximation of 
7 
the expected average weather in a short-term forecast. The ten-year average (2010-2019) 
8 
annual heating degree days for the East Gate is 6,197 versus the Company’s 30-year 
9 
average of 6,378.  Similarly, the ten-year average (2010-2019) annual heating degree 
10 
days for the West Gate is 6,398 versus the Company’s 30-year average of 6,494. 
11 
12 
Nonetheless, the charts included in my response to IRDPS-837 (attached hereto as 
13 
Exhibit __ (TEP-2R)) indicate that the East Gate averages show a clear declining trend in 
14 
annual heating degree days while the West Gate averages showed a declining trend only 
15 
until 2013.  Since then, the West Gate trend has been flat.  Additionally, those two charts 
16 
demonstrate the higher volatility that can occur using only ten years of data instead of 
17 
thirty years of data, including years where the average can increase. 
18 
19 
For all these reasons, the Company does not support any change to its methodology for 
20 
developing its forecast of deliveries, and it continues to believe that using the forecast 
21 
presented in its corrections and updates testimony is reasonable.  Moreover, the Company 
22 
believes it is unreasonable to rely on a ten-year weather pattern for planning its system 
23 


--- Page 9 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
Page 7 of 7 
requirements.  The volatility of using a ten-year dataset for establishing its design day 
1 
and design year would unreasonably lessen the reliability of its resource planning and 
2 
distribution system planning. 
3 
4 
IV. 
Conclusion
5 
Q.
Please summarize your conclusions. 
6 
A.
My conclusions are that the Company’s deliveries forecast is the superior forecast for 
7 
establishing rates in the Rate Year and Data Years.  
8 
9 
Q.
Does that conclude your rebuttal testimony? 
10 
A.
Yes.

Exhibit __ (TEP-1R)
Page 1 of 11
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation
d/b/a National Grid
Case 20-G-0381
A Discussion of the Differences Between the Company’s Gas Deliveries Forecast as
Presented in Its Corrections and Updates and the Staff’s Gas Deliveries Forecast
I.
The Company’s Customer Deliveries Forecast
The Company’s corrections and updates forecast is an econometric forecast that relates the
changes observed in two appropriate variables, historical meter count and use-per-customer, for
five major rate groups to key economic variables.
The product of meter count and use-per-
customer provided the Company’s volume or deliveries forecast. This forecast:

analyzes, separately, the Company’s Eastern (‘East Gate’) and Central (‘West Gate’)
Divisions;

relies on the same April 2020 Moody’s Analytics economic forecast which the
Company believes captures the anticipated impact of the COVID-19 pandemic and
recovery on the Upstate New York regional economies;

updates the historical data through July 2020, reflecting the depth of the COVID
lockdown;

accounts for the anticipated impact of energy efficiency programs and electrification
of heat; and,

adjusts for the Company’s proposed reassignment of certain customers to other service
classifications.
The Company’s forecasting methodology is to develop as thorough and complete a view
of the future as possible by determining the causal relationships between key economic variables


--- Page 13 ---
Exhibit __ (TEP-1R)
Page 2 of 11
and the meter counts and the uses-per-customer for five groups (Residential Non-Heating,
Residential Heating, Commercial, Industrial, and Other) that encompass all of its customers by
choosing the most appropriate methodology as dictated by economic and econometric theory. The
key economic variables evaluated for each of its models are chosen from a larger set of economic
variables provided by Moody’s Analytics based on economic theory and their statistical robustness
in the models.
After the Company developed its forecasting equations, it tested them by
substituting Moody’s high and low economic forecasts to ensure that the models all conform to
economic theory. This form of forecasting is referred to as explanatory modeling.
II.
The Staff’s Customer Gas Deliveries Forecast
In producing its forecast, Staff followed a process of creating an econometric forecast using
the same Company data updated through July 2020 and related the changes observed in historical
meter count and either use-per-customer or volume to key economic variables. Staff did analyze,
separately, the Company’s two divisions. Staff also made the same two post-model adjustments
reflecting (1) energy efficiency and electrification of heat, and (2) the Company’s proposed
reassignment of certain customers to other service classifications. These two adjustments were
reviewed and deemed to be reasonable by Ms. Yezzi (Yezzi at 7).
The material difference between the two forecasts is in the creation of the econometric
forecasts. Staff:

selectively produced forecasts for only certain of the Company’s rate groups; and,

relied on the September 2020 update to Moody’s economic forecast.
It is the method that Staff used to develop its forecast that causes the Company to believe
that its own forecast is more appropriate and should be used for ratemaking purposes in this
proceeding.


--- Page 14 ---
Exhibit __ (TEP-1R)
Page 3 of 11
Additionally, Staff included the use of a rolling 10-year average of weather data for the
development of normal heating and billing degree days for ratemaking purposes as opposed to the
Company’s 30-year average.
III.
The Company’s Issues With Staff’s Forecast Methodology
The forecast proposed by Ms. Yezzi is not a complete forecast for a number of reasons.

First, Ms. Yezzi developed meter count models only for the Residential Heating and
Commercial rate groups. Ms. Yezzi accepted the Company’s forecasts for the other
three rate groups (Residential Non-Heating, Industrial, and Other), implying that these
groups were ‘less conducive to econometric modelling.’ (Yezzi at 5)

Second, producing their models for Residential Heating (for both East Gate and West
Gate) in particular, Ms. Yezzi used no economic variables to explain meter count
changes, something for which she appeared to criticize the Company, relying only on
historical trends as well as autoregressive and moving average variables (Yezzi at 15).
These autoregressive and moving average terms will tend to make future growth look
like past growth and they are used throughout all the forecasts proposed by Ms. Yezzi.
This form of forecasting is referred to as predictive modeling. While auto regressive
and moving average terms can be useful in short-term forecasting as they capture the
most recent dynamics of the variable being modeled via its past values and the error
terms, it is not an appropriate methodology for long-term forecasting into the Rate Year
and the Data Years.
In addition, the causal relationship between the explanatory
variable(s) and the dependent variable is muddled in the presence of autoregressive and
moving average terms.


--- Page 15 ---
Exhibit __ (TEP-1R)
Page 4 of 11

Third, in their models of volumes and use-per-customer, Staff did not develop
separate models for the peak and off-peak periods.” The Company did produce
separate models because the two periods reflect significantly different usage
patterns: with and without heating equipment being used.
Failing to develop
separate models could lead to understating the peak period usage and overstating
the off-peak period usage.
Predictive modeling is useful for short-term forecasting, such as day-ahead forecasting
when economic conditions are stable, but its use is questionable for long-term forecasting driven
by macroeconomics. This is particularly important during the period of the COVID-19 pandemic
and recovery. This can be seen in the comparison charts of Residential meter count forecasts
(Residential Heating forecasts plus Company Residential Non-Heating forecast) in Figure 1 where
the East Gate and West Gate forecasts by Staff are greater than the Company’s Corrections &
Updates forecasts and they appear to merely follow the trend of the pre-COVID data. Conversely,
the Company’s forecasts are driven by the forecasted number of households (East Gate) and time
and personal income (West Gate).
Figure 1

This underprediction of Commercial meters is again striking given that the Staff forecast
was based on the September 2020 Moody’s forecast (Attachment to DPS_838) versus the
Company forecast which used the April 2020 Moody’s forecast. As shown in Figure 4, dividing
regional GDP by its Commercial meter count shows that both Moody’s forecasts of per-unit GDP
converge by 2022.
Figure 4
As noted above, in producing the volume forecasts that they did present, Staff relied on a
use-per-customer model for East Gate and West Gate Residential Heating.
For their volume
forecasts of East Gate and West Gate Commercial and Other rate groups, Staff modeled volume
directly which should be considered inappropriate because these models confound the two
dependent variables (meter count and use-per-customer) into one and, thus, any independent
variables (e.g. billing degree days, economic variables) must account for the two separate
dependent variables. In all cases, Staff’s forecast not only uses billing degree day variable to
describe the seasonality of use-per-customer, but also continues to use autoregressive and, in the
case of the Other rate group, moving average terms which can lead to extending the impact of the
COVID pandemic further into the Rate Year and Data Years.

Extending the impact of the COVID pandemic further into the Rate Year and beyond can
be seen in Staff’s West Gate Residential Heating use-per-customer forecast (Figure 5). While the
Company’s and Staff’s forecasts of East Gate Residential Heating use-per-customer are similar
due to the lack of a trend in its historical data, the rising historical trend in the West Gate
Residential Heating use-per-customer appears to bias the Staff forecast higher than the Company’s
forecasted values due to the autoregressive and moving average terms.
Figure 5
For their volume forecasts of East Gate and West Gate Commercial rate group, Staff
modeled volume directly, which the Company considers inappropriate as these models confound
the two dependent variables (meter count and use-per-customer). In Figure 6 below, the Company
has divided the Staff volume forecast by the corresponding meter count forecast to compare its
Commercial use-per-customer forecasts to the implicit Staff forecasts. In their East Gate and West
Gate forecasts, Staff’s forecasts fall lower than the Company’s forecasts and never demonstrate a
recovery to pre-pandemic levels while the Company’s forecasts clearly show a recovery.

For their volume forecasts of East Gate and West Gate Other rate group, Staff modeled
volume directly, which the Company considers inappropriate as these models confound the two
dependent variables (meter count and use-per-customer). In Figure 7 below, the Company has
divided the Staff volume forecast by the corresponding meter count forecast to compare its Other
use-per-customer forecasts to the implicit Staff forecasts. In both cases, the Staff forecasts appear
to have significantly different view of the future versus the Company forecasts.
Its East Gate
forecast recovers more quickly from the pandemic but does not align with the Company’s East
Gate forecast until 2023. Meanwhile, its West Gate forecast shows significantly more volatility
than that which was observed in the past decade, falling further due to the pandemic and then
abruptly reversing course to significantly exceed the Company’s West Gate forecast by 2023.

Figure 7
IV.
Response To Staff’s Technical Criticisms Of The Company’s Forecast
While Staff witness Yezzi expresses concern (at 9) that several of the Company’s delivery
models lack relevant explanatory independent variables other than weather, out of the thirty
models that the Company produced for its forecast (ten meter count models and twenty use-per-
customer models), Staff pointed out only three models that relied on weather as the only
explanatory variable and one model that did not rely on an econometric model. These four models
were all use-per-customer models. As noted above, the Company created two separate use-per-
customer models for each rate group, peak period and off-peak period, to properly model its
monthly usage data when heating equipment is either on or off because the usage patterns will be
significantly different.
The two Company use-per-customer models for East Gate Residential Heating, peak and
off-peak, used only billing degree days as their explanatory variables. There was no other
economic variable (nor the time variable) that was found to add additionally explanatory power to
its model. Therefore, the Company did not add other variables. In modeling East Gate Residential
Heating use-per-customer without separating peak and off-peak periods, Staff’s model was able to
produce one model with the billing degree day variable and added two more economic variables


--- Page 21 ---
Exhibit __ (TEP-1R)
Page 10 of 11
plus five seasonal variables, one dummy, and an autoregressive term. The coefficient of at least
one of the economic variables seems to be so small that, while it may correlate to use-per-customer,
it may contribute very little explanatory power.
The Company’s use-per-customer model for the peak period West Gate Other rate group
used billing degree days as its explanatory variable. However, the Company found that there was
no other economic variable that contributed to its model. As noted by Staff, (rate) groups with
fewer and more varied types of customers are less conducive to econometric modelling than large
customer groups of generally homogenous customers. (Yezzi at 5).
Lastly, because the Company found that there was no economic variable that contributed
to the forecasts for their off-peak period East Gate Other rate group, the Company was unable to
develop a use-per-customer model for the group.
The Company assumed that the use-per-
customer pattern in the most-recent historical period would be most reasonable to use for the
forecasted values. The Company’s volume forecast is then the product of this use-per-customer
forecast and the corresponding meter count forecast. This multiplication means that the volume
forecast does have an economic driver: that used in the meter count forecast.
V.
Conclusion
In summary, the Company’s conclusions are:
1.
Time series models which include autoregressive and moving average terms have
limited scope in terms of how far into the future they should be used for forecasting and are
inappropriate as Staff has applied them here;
2.
Staff’s Residential Heating meter count models do not include any economic
explanatory variables, which undermines Staff’s critique of the Company’s forecast in this regard;
and,


--- Page 22 ---
Exhibit __ (TEP-1R)
Page 11 of 11
3.
Modeling volumes as is done in Staff’s deliveries forecast is inappropriate. Use-
per-customer and meter count should be the terms that are modeled and volumes should be derived
from their product.

Rebuttal Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (TEP-2R):  
Billing Degree Day data and Analysis for 10-year Weather Normalization for the East and West 
 Gate regions (previously submitted in response to Information Request (“IR”) DPS-837) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


--- Page 24 ---
Form 103 
Date of Request:  October 5, 2020 
Request No. DPS-837 
Due Date:  October 15, 2020 
NMPC Req. No. NM-1081 
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION d/b/a NATIONAL GRID 
Case Nos. 20-E-0380 and 20-G-0381 
Electric and Gas Rates 
Request for Information 
FROM: 
DPS Staff, McKenzie Yezzi 
TO: 
National Grid, Theodore Poe 
SUBJECT:  
Weather Normalization BDD Data 
Request: 
Note: 
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall 
be construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet 
models in original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
1.
Provide the source data and workpapers used to develop the BDD variable used in the
Company’s UPC models.  Describe the methodology and data transformations performed
to calculate the BDD variable.
2.
Re-calculate and provide the BDD variable used in the Company’s UPC models based on
a 10-year rather than 30-year HDD normalization.
3.
Update the 10-year time period used to calculate BDD to include the most recent 10 years
2010-2019.
Response 
1.
As described on page six of my direct testimony, the  Company  prepared its  gas
delivery  forecast  using econometric/statistical  forecast  models developed for two
geographic regions (“East Gate” and “West Gate”) and five major groupings of its rate
classes (“Customer Groups”).  The Company’s West Gate region is Amsterdam  and  the
area  west  of  Amsterdam,  including  Syracuse  and  Watertown.  The Company’s East
Gate region is east of Amsterdam.
Attachment 1 and Attachment 2 contain the source data and workpapers that the
Company used to develop its historical and forecast monthly values for heating degree
days (“HDD”) and billing degree days (“BDD”) for the Company’s East Gate and West
Gate regions, respectively.  The process followed is the same in both Attachments; the
data differs because East Gate uses weather data from the Albany International Airport
(KALB) and West Gate uses weather data from the Syracuse Hancock International
Airport (KSYR).
Exhibit __ (TEP-2R)  
Page 1 of 3


--- Page 25 ---
Form 103 
In performing its retail gas forecast, the Company used its historical billing data to 
determine monthly meter counts and monthly volumes for the five rate groups that it 
models.  The Company modeled meter counts and use-per-customer (volume divided by 
meter counts).  Billing degree day data was used as an independent variable in the 
Company’s use-per-customer modeling because usage in most of the Company’s rate 
groups is driven primarily by the weather.  Because the billing data is gathered in a 
continuous operation throughout the month, the billing data will lag the Company’s 
actual monthly gas supply purchases.  Because of this lag in billing, monthly HDD data 
will not provide the best correlation with the monthly billing use-per-customer.  To 
provide a better correlation of usage and weather, billing degree day data is calculated 
from the daily HDD data by offsetting HDD data by  a half-month.  (For example, HDD 
for March of a given year would include data from a portion of February and a portion of 
March.)  This best approximates the degree days that would have occurred, on average, 
during the billing month. 
Attachment 1 and Attachment 2 set forth the development of the historical actual BDD 
and forecast normal BDD used in the Company’s use-per-customer modeling. 
The actual daily HDD is in range E5:E5939.  The Company’s daily 30-year normal HDD 
is in range J5:J5939.  Monthly totals are calculated in range E5943:J6137.  The offsetting 
of the daily HDD data is in range M5943:V6137. 
2.
Attachment 3 is an Excel spreadsheet that contains two tabs, one for KALB and one for
KSYR.  It contains the daily historical HDD data for the two weather stations in columns
F:BB.  This data has been used to calculate the Company’s 30-year normal HDD data for
its modeling purposes.  The Company uses a rolling 30 years of most recent HDD data to
allow stability in its normal and, at the same time, to reflect any long-term changes in
climate in the two locations.
Using these two datasets, in column CD of each tab, the Company has calculated the ten-
year averages from 1990-1999 through 2010-2019.  Both charts show that ten-year
averages have greater volatility than what would be expected with 30-year averages.
Chart 1 below shows KALB ten-year averages.  Chart 2 shows the KSYR ten-year
averages.  The KALB averages show a clear declining trend while the KSYR averages
show a declining trend until 2013.  Since then, the KSYR trend has been flat.
Exhibit __ (TEP-2R)  
Page 2 of 3


--- Page 26 ---
Form 103 
3.
The table below shows the normal (non-leap year) BDDs used in the Company’s
corrections and updates forecast (30-year average from 1990-2019) and the normal (non-
leap year) BDDs based on a ten-year average using data from 2010-2019.  Because
BDDs are lagged HDDs, the normal annual totals for either will be the same. (BDDs for a
given year would omit the last 15 days of December in the given year but would include
the last 15 days of December in the prior year.)
